| message type 0, a code in 20 to 31 that this section leaves unassigned, or a code the receiving module does not expect (Control message type codes) | drop and count under BadLength `[decision]` — provisional, as the Hello section rules; confirmed by 004-21 (Hello, Open items) |
| oversize: a datagram larger than 1 252 bytes (C-17) | drop and count under OversizeControl |
| datagram size below the fixed part of its message type — 79 bytes for a request, 56 for a manifest section — or other than the fixed size of an accept (62) or a refusal (53) | drop and count under BadLength: the body does not fill the fixed size `[decision]` |
| authentication scheme non-zero | drop and count under ReservedNonZero (Reserved fields, Authentication reservation (C-12)) |
| authentication tag non-zero | drop and count under ReservedNonZero (Reserved fields, Authentication reservation (C-12)) |
| recipient module id other than the receiving module's own | drop and count under BadLength `[decision]` — provisional: the message is not one the receiving module expects, as for an unexpected message type; confirmed by 004-21 (Open items) |
| an accept, refusal or manifest section whose request id names no handshake the receiver holds with that sender | not a parse rejection: the message is well formed, and matching it needs the admission machine's per-handshake state, which no packet rule holds; the admission machine decides (epic 005, 005-14) `[decision]` |

No value of a module id, the request id, a port id, a session or the message counter is a malformed case: each accepts every value its type holds, and only the rules above and the per-message rules reject `[decision]`. Every rule is decidable from the datagram's own bytes and the receiver's own module id in one pass (C-164), except the last row, which is the admission machine's `[decision]`. The Rejection reasons enumeration is unchanged: every outcome above names an existing member whose Layers cell already lists the control layer, or is a refusal of the separate enumeration, or is not a rejection `[decision]`.

### Open items

**Open item 1 — the admission message set.** The set is four messages: the admission request (code 16), the admission accept (17), the admission refusal (18) and the manifest section (19); codes 20 to 31 stay unused, the path-MTU probe pair travels in 004-27's codes 32 to 35, operator cancel sends nothing (C-45, 012-24), and the request travels from the source side, epic 012's requester. Owner: epic 004 proposes it here; epic 005 confirms it — 005-13 names the admission messages in its input alphabet and 005-14 writes their rows. Two landed tickets already disagree with the set, and the disagreement is recorded here rather than left as a hypothesis: 005-13 expects "the operator-cancel message" from epic 004 and lists operator cancel among its inputs, while this set has none, because C-45's cancel is a local input on a Pending request that has sent nothing (012-24) — owner 005-13, which turns it into a local input; and 012-01's `Declared` carries a buffer memory and a cost bound, while the request carries neither, because the receiver computes buffer memory from A and the rates (C-96) and the cost bound is the consumer's own or travels in the manifest (004-26) — owner 012-01, with 004-26. Falsifier: epic 005's admission state machine needs a message or field the set lacks — for example a requester other than the source side (a consumer or a third party) that must start a handshake, an operator cancel or a teardown that must reach the peer, or a path-MTU result in the accept `[decision]`.

**Open item 2 — provisional BadLength outcomes.** A zero or inconsistent declared rate, an invalid pinned address, a zero port or intended packet size, an unknown refusal reason, a refusal carrying a discovery-raised reason and a recipient module id that is not the receiver's are counted under BadLength, the enumeration's declared-shape member, provisionally. Owner: ticket 004-21 (validity rules completeness audit), which also consolidates the order of the shared and per-message rules. Falsifier: 004-21 finds a member of the closed enumeration that names one of these cases better, or rules that one of them is not a rejection `[decision]`.

**Open item 3 — how the declared rate is computed.** C-219 and Size budget, Wire size and capacity recompute, declare a stream sent one packet per slice with its headers at the 100-byte cap — 1 318 bytes, 21 088 000 bit/s, for an audio stream at the defaults — while 010-05 sums each packet's real UDP payload plus 28 + 38 bytes, about 20.22 Mbit/s for the default 1 198-byte packet, so the two disagree for one-packet slices already; and no constant and no landed section states the rate of a stream whose slices take several packets. The u64 field carries whatever figure the settled rule gives. Owner: ticket 010-05 (wire rate of a bundle), which computes the figure for admission, with the design owner, who decides whether a constant states it. Falsifier: the source side and the commissioning tool compute different declared rates for the same stream, one-packet or multi-packet, or a stream sends more bit/s on the wire than it declared `[decision]`.

**Open item 4 — a second request for an admitted bundle.** A consumer module holds at most one admitted connection per bundle of a source, because the receiver report keys a connection by the reporter identity and the bundle; whether a second request for the same consumer module and bundle replaces the first as a re-admission or is refused, and with which reason, is not decided here. Owner: ticket 005-14 (admission handshake transition table), with 005-15 (connection states, including the producer's restart after re-admission); the closing of a replaced reverse port is a hand-off added to 005-15 here, since its text does not mention reverse ports. Falsifier: epic 005 admits two concurrent connections of one consumer module to one bundle, whose receiver reports a producer then cannot tell apart (Receiver report, Bundle identity and member rows) `[decision]`.

**Open item 5 — the manifest envelope and port names.** The manifest section's envelope — sender, recipient, request id, section index and count, length — is proposed here, and which side sends its manifest and when is left to 004-26 with epic 005; Hello, Open item 9 asks 004-18 for a way to read a port's name before naming the port, and this set offers none beyond the manifest sections of a handshake. Manifest keeps the envelope unchanged and lays out the port-to-stream mapping: the consumer decides an unknown member (`BadMemberList`) against the source's manifest, so that decision waits for the source's manifest, while refusals decidable without it do not wait (Manifest, Which side sends it, and when; Open item 3). The port-name half of this item's falsifier is met: no message lets a requester read a port's name outside a handshake, which Manifest, Open item 4 records with its owners. Owner: ticket 004-26 (manifest encoding), with 005-13 for when manifests are exchanged. Falsifier: 004-26 needs an envelope field the section lacks, a manifest keyed other than by handshake, or a manifest larger than 255 sections; or a requester cannot learn a port's name and id before it names the port `[decision]`.

**Open item 6 — the probe pair, the path MTU and the reverse port at the consumer.** The request carries the intended packet size and a request id that the probe pair may use; which side probes, how the probe names its handshake and whether the accept carries the path MTU are 004-27's; Path-MTU probe pair answers them: the source side probes, each probe and reply carry the request id, and no admission message carries the result (its Open item 2). Epic 012's shape (012-08, 012-13) has the consumer allocate a reverse port and return it with the path MTU in its `Accept`, while the landed Receiver report and One-way probe sections send reports and probe requests to the reverse port the producer gave; this layout follows the landed sections and puts the reverse port in the request. 012-13 also pins the request datagram's source address as the source, and 012-08's `Accept` carries a `pinned_source`, while here the request carries the pinned source address and the consumer pins that field (Who sends what). Owner: ticket 004-27 (path-MTU probe pair) for the probe binding and result; ticket 012-08 (handshake message codec) with 012-13 for the `Accept` shape. Falsifier: 004-27 needs a field in the request or the accept that this layout lacks, or epic 012 requires a consumer-side reverse port that the landed measurement sections do not use, or a source pinned from the datagram rather than from the field `[decision]`.

**Open item 7 — where ForeignDomain and FollowerCap arise.** C-185 marks only three reasons as raised at discovery, yet a foreign-domain request is dropped and counted under the rejection ForeignDomain by the shared rules above, as every foreign-domain control message is, so the refusal ForeignDomain is never sent from a parsed request; epic 005 places ForeignDomain in discovery, and a conforming source side never sends one, since the target's foreign hellos are dropped and it is never present. FollowerCap is a clock holder's refusal (C-35), produced by no stream admission check (012-06), and no landed message carries it to a follower. Owner: ticket 005-21 (refusal scenarios), with 012-06 (peer identity and cap checks) and 005-04 (control enumerations). Falsifier: epic 005 needs an admission refusal with reason ForeignDomain sent in answer to a request, or a FollowerCap refusal that must reach the follower in a message no section lays out `[decision]`.

**Open item 8 — the downstream A carries the consumer's current A.** The accept's downstream A is the consumer's current A as its receiver reports carry it — its own group's A, the A it inherited, or its configured budget — so the value at admission and the value after admission have one meaning; C-142 names only "the A of the downstream presentation group", and whether a configured budget, or the A relayed through a module that aligns nothing (Receiver report, Open item 4), is inherited upstream is not settled by C-142 or C-198. Owner: ticket 005-27 (fan-in A and alignment opt-out), with 005-28 (changed A propagation). Falsifier: epic 005 rules that a fan-in upstream inherits only a presentation group's A and never a configured budget, so the accept and the receiver report must carry two different values `[decision]`.

**Open item 9 — hand-offs to epic 012's types and codec.** Four points of epic 012 differ from this layout. 012-01 and 012-05 treat an empty member list as the whole output (012-05 AC 4), which this layout retires: every requested stream is listed, and a member count of 0 is dropped under BadLength. 012-08 says `RefuseReason` has 16 variants, while C-185 and 012-01 have 18; its codec must reject the reason values 8, 13 and 14 in a refusal as well as 18 and above, and a member count of 0, and it refuses a count of 33 or more from the count byte whatever the length. 012-05 names no reason for a source port id that names no output; this layout refuses it with BadMemberList. The `RefuseReason` discriminants of 012-01, 0 to 17 in C-185's order, are the values of Refusal reasons above. Owner: ticket 012-01 (core admission types), with 012-05 (manifest and bundle member checks) and 012-08 (handshake message codec). Falsifier: epic 012 needs the whole-output shorthand, a reason other than BadMemberList for a missing output port, or `RefuseReason` values other than 0 to 17 in C-185's order `[decision]`.

## Manifest

The manifest is the module's declaration that admission and the receivers of its streams read: its ports, with their direction, content types, names and channel counts; its bundles; its streams, with each one's output port, role, sample format, slice length, channel map, controllers and flags; its structural latency L and the delays the compiler inserted at joins; the conversion delay of each device-facing port; its cost bound; the required E of its presentation group and its own declared E `[decision]`. It travels only in the manifest sections of an admission handshake (Admission messages, Manifest section), which are its envelope; this section lays out the bytes those sections carry, fixes every maximum count, states the manifest's maximum size against the envelope and the control bound (C-17), and gives every invalid form with its outcome `[decision]`.

Offsets in this section's tables are byte distances from the manifest's first byte — the first manifest byte of the section with index 0 — not from a UDP payload, because the manifest is the concatenation of its sections' manifest bytes in index order (Admission messages, Manifest section) `[decision]`. The manifest is little-endian (Byte order) and packed with no padding (Alignment and padding), and every list is a count followed by that many elements with a fixed maximum (Field-table format, rule 2) `[decision]`. The fixed part comes first and holds every count; the lists follow in the order of the table, each starting directly after the previous list's last element, so the offsets of the counted rows are those of a manifest at its maximum counts, the bound a parser sizes for, as the hello's are (Hello, Hello layout) `[decision]`.

Where each content comes from `[decision]`:

- The ticket's rulings: ports and content types (Port element); the channel map (C-04; Lists); L in whole samples (C-105; Manifest layout); the inserted join delays with the frequency-dependent-delay flag (C-104; Join element); dense controller initial values (C-158; Lists); the conversion delay on the device-facing port (C-207; Port element); the cost bound in operations per slice (C-106) and required and declared E (C-103, C-140; E and the cost bound) `[decision]`.
- What landed sections hand to the manifest: the port id as an index into the manifest's port list (Hello, Port element) and the port's name (Hello, Name and Port element); the bundle's group id and member list (Hello, Bundle element); each stream's sample format (Bit-level encoding of samples; Content messages, Audio message), role (Content messages, Content region), controller-map order (Dense controller message), sparse field map with initial values (Sparse keyed entry) and transport-state declaration (Transport state message); whether a source measures a stream (Size budget, Open item (measuring declaration)); a member's slice length (Admission messages, Declared rates); and which streams belong to which output port (Admission messages, The member list and the hello's bundles). Each has a field below `[decision]`.
- What the manifest does not carry, and why, is recorded in Open items 6 and 7: channel names, controller value ranges, and the compiler's own manifest items that admission does not read `[decision]`.

### Manifest layout

The fixed part is 31 bytes: L, the cost bound, the two E values and the seven counts; the seven lists follow `[decision]`:

| Offset (bytes) | Width (bytes) | Type | Meaning | Valid range |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 4 | u32 | Structural latency L of the module (C-105): whole samples at the domain rate, 96 000 Hz (C-01); the longest path, padding included (C-104); never a conversion delay (C-207) | 0 to 67 108 863 samples |
| 4 | 8 | u64 | Cost bound (C-106): counted abstract operations per slice, identical on every machine; admission converts it with the calibration factor of the regime stamp (004-25) | 0 to 18 446 744 073 709 551 614 operations per slice; 0xffffffffffffffff = none: the module is not a DSL program and declares no counted cost bound |
| 12 | 4 | u32 | Required E (C-103, C-73): the E the module's presentation group requires of its sinks, in nanoseconds | 0 to 4 294 967 293 ns, an E the group requires; 0xffffffff = none: the group sets no required E; 0xfffffffe is not valid in this field |
| 16 | 4 | u32 | Declared E (C-103, C-140, C-73): the module's own E as a sink, in nanoseconds | 0 to 4 294 967 293 ns, the certified E of a commissioned sink; 0xfffffffe = unknown: no certified E; 0xffffffff is not valid in this field |
| 20 | 1 | u8 | Port count: how many port elements follow | 0 to 51 |
| 21 | 1 | u8 | Bundle count: how many bundle elements follow the port elements | 0 to 8 |
| 22 | 2 | u16 | Stream count: how many stream elements follow the bundle elements | 0 to 256 (C-09) |
| 24 | 1 | u8 | Join-delay count: how many inserted join delays (C-104) follow the stream elements | 0 to 255 |
| 25 | 2 | u16 | Channel-map entry count: the entries of every stream's channel map together, the sum of the stream elements' channel counts | 0 to 1 536 |
| 27 | 2 | u16 | Dense controller count: the dense controllers of every stream together, the sum of the stream elements' dense counts | 0 to 1 024 |
| 29 | 2 | u16 | Sparse field count: the sparse fields of every stream together, the sum of the stream elements' sparse counts | 0 to 1 024 |
| 31 | 45 × max 51 | byte blob of port elements | Port: a port element, 9 + content-type count + name length bytes and at most 45, laid out as Port element fixes; the port id is the element's index, 0 first | port count elements, at most 51 (2 295 bytes) |
| 2326 | 34 × max 8 | byte blob of bundle elements | Bundle: a bundle element, 2 + member count bytes and at most 34, laid out as Bundle element fixes | bundle count elements, at most 8 (272 bytes) |
| 2598 | 15 × max 256 | byte blob of stream elements | Stream: a stream element, 15 bytes, laid out as Stream element fixes | stream count elements, at most 256 (3 840 bytes) |
| 6438 | 5 × max 255 | byte blob of join elements | Join delay: an inserted join delay in whole samples with its frequency-dependent-delay flag, laid out as Join element fixes | join-delay count elements, at most 255 (1 275 bytes) |
| 7713 | 2 × max 1536 | u16 | Channel map (C-04): one port channel index per entry, every stream's entries in stream-element order (Lists) | channel-map entry count entries, at most 1 536; each below its output port's channel count, none twice within one output |
| 10785 | 4 × max 1024 | f32 or Q1.31, per the stream's sample format | Dense controller initial value (C-158): one per dense controller, every stream's in stream-element order, each stream's in controller-map order (Lists) | dense controller count values, at most 1 024; any 32-bit pattern; 0 is the default |
| 14881 | 4 × max 1024 | f32 or Q1.31, per the stream's sample format | Sparse field initial value: one per field of a stream's sparse field map, every stream's in stream-element order, the field id being the index within its stream (Lists) | sparse field count values, at most 1 024; any 32-bit pattern |

The maximum manifest size is 18 977 bytes, every count at its maximum: 31 + 51 × 45 + 8 × 34 + 256 × 15 + 255 × 5 + 1 536 × 2 + 1 024 × 4 + 1 024 × 4 = 31 + 2 295 + 272 + 3 840 + 1 275 + 3 072 + 4 096 + 4 096 = 18 977 `[decision]`. The smallest manifest is the 31-byte fixed part, a module with no port and no stream `[decision]`. A computed example, not a bound: a source with one 32-member bundle of 31 audio members of 6 channels and one controller member with 16 dense controllers, one output carrying audio and dense controllers and one input carrying audio, each with a 32-byte name and no join, is 31 + 43 + 42 + 34 + 32 × 15 + 186 × 2 + 16 × 4 = 1 066 bytes, one section of 1 122 bytes `[decision]`. The current schema of wire.toml cannot declare the whole manifest as one message, because wire-check bounds every control message at 1 252 bytes and has no notion of a payload joined from several sections, and this tooling gap is owned by ticket 004-23, the epic close-out that holds the "machine-readable tables parse and round-trip" item of the definition of done; wire.toml declares the 31-byte fixed part and the four element tables `[decision]`. On the wire the lists follow one another with no gap: the first port element at byte 31, each element directly after the one before it, the first element of each list directly after the last element of the previous list, and the manifest ends with its last sparse initial value `[decision]`.

L is a u32 with a maximum of 67 108 863 samples, about 699 s, because the measurement extension adds the L of up to 64 processors into a u32 of samples: 64 × 67 108 863 = 4 294 967 232 stays below 2^32, so the declared-delay field cannot saturate over the maximum hop count at the largest L a manifest admits, which is the falsifier Measurement extension, Open item records `[decision]`. A module whose L exceeds the maximum has no manifest form: as a DSL program it is refused at compile time by the DSL front end (C-218), and any other module refuses to start (Open item 2) `[decision]`.

### Port element

A port is one of the module's inputs or outputs; its port id is its index in the manifest's port list, the id the hello's port element and the admission request carry (Hello, Port element; Admission messages, Admission request) `[decision]`. Offsets are relative to the element's first byte; the sub-byte fields of byte 0 are numbered as Bit-field numbering fixes `[decision]`:

| Offset (bytes) | Width (bytes) | Type | Meaning | Valid range |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 1 bit | uint | Direction, bit 0 of byte 0: 0 = input, 1 = output, the hello's encoding | 0 or 1 |
| 0 | 1 bit | uint | Device-facing, bit 1 of byte 0: 1 = the port faces a hardware device, an edge module's capture input or playback output (C-207); 0 = a network port | 0 or 1 |
| 0 | 6 bits | uint | Reserved, bits 7..2 of byte 0 (Reserved fields, General rule for later tickets) | 0 only |
| 1 | 4 | u32 | Conversion delay of a device-facing port (C-207): the delay of the edge's fixed-ratio resampler node (C-189, C-134), in whole samples at the domain rate, 96 000 Hz (C-01) | any 32-bit value on a device-facing port; 0 only on a port that is not device-facing |
| 5 | 2 | u16 | Channel count: how many audio channels the port carries, numbered 0 to count − 1; for an output that stream elements name, the number of channel-map entries its streams carry | 0 to 1 536 |
| 7 | 1 | u8 | Content-type count: how many content types the port carries | 1 to 4 |
| 8 | 1 | u8 | Name length: how many bytes of name follow the content types | 1 to 32 |
| 9 | 1 × max 4 | u8 | Content types: one content-message kind code per content type the port carries (Content messages, Kind codes), each at most once, the hello's codes | content-type count codes, at most 4; codes 0 to 3 in v1, a receiver ignores any other code |
| 13 | 1 × max 32 | u8 | Name: the port's name, UTF-8 (RFC 3629) as the module name is (Hello, Name) | valid UTF-8; 1 to 32 bytes |

A port element is 9 + content-type count + name length bytes, at most 9 + 4 + 32 = 45; on the wire the name follows the last content type directly `[decision]`. At most 51 ports, the hello's maximum, so every port of the manifest has a hello port element and the two lists agree port id for port id; direction and content types are the hello's encodings, and the manifest is their authority (Hello, Port element) `[decision]`. The name is the port's full name, the one the hello leaves to the manifest, with the module name's rules: UTF-8, compared byte for byte, at most 32 bytes (Hello, Name); a content-type code above 3 is ignored, as in the hello, so a content domain a minor version adds can appear in a port `[decision]`.

The conversion delay's unit is whole samples at the domain rate, its width 32 bits `[decision]`. The manifest reports the edge's fixed-ratio resampler node: its ratio is 2:1 or 1:2 and its delay a whole number of samples (C-189, C-105), and it may appear only inside an edge module (C-134), so the delay is a compile-time constant carried exactly, never counted in L (C-207) `[decision]`. The edge's rate actuator is a separate, steerable resampler whose ratio varies by parts per million (C-189), and its delay is part of D (C-170); this layout does not report it, and 018-11 reads it from the actuator itself; a device-facing port with no resampler node, a device at 96 000 Hz, reports 0 `[decision]`. 018-11 and 018-27 count the actuator inside the conversion delay, in nanoseconds; that disagreement is Open item 5 `[decision]`.

The device-facing flag is what the conversion delay is checked against: a non-zero conversion delay on a port whose flag is 0 is invalid (Validity rules) `[decision]`. A device-facing port carries no stream: no stream element names it (Stream element) `[decision]`. No landed rule refuses a request whose consumer port id names a device-facing input — NotAnInput applies only to a port that is not an input — and that case is handed to 012-05 and 005-14 (Open item 3) `[decision]`. The reserved bits follow the general rule, not the hello's accept-any rule: the hello is a discovery hint that a minor version may extend without a v1 receiver losing the peer, while the manifest declares what a receiver must honour to admit and parse the streams, so a property it cannot read is dropped and counted, never ignored (Reserved fields, General rule for later tickets; Reserved fields, Fields of the manifest) `[decision]`. The consequence: the manifest has no extension point a minor version could use — its reserved bits are rejected and it carries no length a receiver could skip by — so every addition to the manifest other than a new content-type code is a major change, a content-type code from 4 to 255 being accept-any so that a minor version can add a content domain (Versioning, Minor and major changes; Reserved fields, Fields of the manifest) `[decision]`.

### Bundle element

A bundle is a group id, a member list and a channel map, declared in the manifest (01-architecture.md § 7; ADR 02); the bundle element carries the group id and the member list, and each member's channel map is in its stream element and the channel-map list `[decision]`:

| Offset (bytes) | Width (bytes) | Type | Meaning | Valid range |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 1 | u8 | Group id: the bundle's identity within its source (01-architecture.md § 7), the hello's group id | 0 to 255; distinct among the manifest's bundles |
| 1 | 1 | u8 | Member count: how many members the bundle has, the controller member included (C-08, C-209) | 1 to 32 |
| 2 | 1 × max 32 | u8 | Member list: the stream number (C-10) of each member, in the order of the hello's member list | member count entries, at most 32; each the stream number of a stream element of the manifest, distinct |

A bundle element is 2 + member count bytes, at most 34 `[decision]`. The group id is 1 byte, the width the hello proposes: a source has at most 256 streams (C-09) and a bundle at least one member, so a source never has more than 256 bundles; this answers Hello, Open item 8 with that width, unique within the source `[decision]`. At most 8 bundles, the hello's maximum, so every bundle of the manifest has a hello bundle element; the hello's per-member channel count is the stream element's channel count, so the two carry one channel split `[decision]`. Every member of one bundle names the same output port in its stream element, and a stream is a member of at most one bundle `[decision]`.

### Stream element

A stream element describes one stream the module sends: which output it belongs to and how its slices are built, read by the consumer that admits it and by the compiler that checks the module (C-181) `[decision]`. The sub-byte fields of byte 2 are numbered as Bit-field numbering fixes `[decision]`:

| Offset (bytes) | Width (bytes) | Type | Meaning | Valid range |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 1 | u8 | Stream number (C-10) of the stream at this source, the 1-byte encoding of the hello's member list | 0 to 255 (C-09); distinct among the stream elements |
| 1 | 1 | u8 | Output port id: the port id of the output port this stream belongs to | the id of a port of the manifest that is an output and not device-facing |
| 2 | 1 bit | uint | Role, bit 0 of byte 2: 0 = audio stream, 1 = controller stream (Content messages, Content region) | 0 or 1 |
| 2 | 1 bit | uint | Sample format, bit 1 of byte 2: 0 = f32, 1 = Q1.31, for audio and every controller value of the stream (C-03; Bit-level encoding of samples) | 0 or 1 |
| 2 | 1 bit | uint | Transport state, bit 2 of byte 2: 1 = the stream carries one transport state message in every non-gap slice (Transport state message) | 0 or 1 |
| 2 | 1 bit | uint | Measured, bit 3 of byte 2: 1 = this source measures the stream and emits the measurement extension on it (Measurement extension, Forwarding update rule) | 0 or 1 |
| 2 | 4 bits | uint | Reserved, bits 7..4 of byte 2 (Reserved fields, General rule for later tickets) | 0 only |
| 3 | 2 | u16 | Frames per slice: the stream's slice length at the domain rate, the frames field of its slices (Slice header) | 1 to 65 535; 48 in the default regime (C-02) |
| 5 | 2 | u16 | Channel count: how many channel-map entries the stream has, one per channel of its audio message | 1 to 1 536 for an audio stream, with frames per slice × channel count × 4 at most 65 535; 0 for a controller stream |
| 7 | 2 | u16 | Dense controller count: how many dense controllers the stream carries, in controller-map order | 0 to 1 024 |
| 9 | 2 | u16 | Sparse field count: how many fields the stream's sparse field map declares | 0 to 256 |
| 11 | 4 | u32 | Controller bytes per slice: the most bytes of controller messages, common headers included, the stream carries in one slice, the c of C-219 | any 32-bit value; 0 for a stream that carries no controller message |

A stream element is 15 bytes, fixed `[decision]`. The output port id is the port-to-stream mapping: the streams of an output are exactly the stream elements that name its port id, so a consumer decides "a stream of the output the source port id names" (Admission messages, Admission request) from the source's manifest, and a listed stream that is not one is an unknown member, refused with `BadMemberList` (012-05, C-173) `[decision]`. A single-stream source's controller second stream (C-181) is a second stream element naming the same output port with role 1; a bundle's controller member (C-205, C-209) is a member stream with role 1 `[decision]`.

The four flags carry what the landed media sections leave to the manifest `[decision]`:

- **Role.** It fixes which content messages a slice of the stream carries (Content messages, Content region): an audio stream has at least one channel and carries the audio message first; a controller stream has no channel and carries at least one dense controller, one sparse field or the transport state; an audio member of a bundle carries no controller of any kind (C-205), so its dense and sparse counts, its transport flag and its controller bytes are 0 `[decision]`.
- **Sample format.** It is the one place the format is signalled, once per stream, for audio and every controller value alike (Content messages, Audio message and Dense controller message); the format value is checked here at admission, never per packet, as Content messages, Validity rules has it `[decision]`.
- **Transport state.** It is the declaration the transport state message rules read: one transport state message in every non-gap slice of a stream that sets it, none in a stream that does not (Transport state message; Validity of sparse keyed and transport state messages) `[decision]`.
- **Measured.** It answers Size budget, Open item (measuring declaration): it states, per stream, whether this source measures the stream, from which the compiler decides with the graph whether the stream could carry the measurement extension (Size budget, Shared packets and the controller allowance) `[decision]`.

A stream's channel count is bounded by the audio message as well: its length is a u16, so frames per slice × channel count × 4 is at most 65 535 bytes — 341 channels at the default 48 frames — and a stream beyond it has no v1 wire form (Content messages, Audio message); a manifest that declares one is invalid (Validity rules) `[decision]`.

Frames per slice is the member's own slice length that Admission messages, Declared rates places in the manifest, the length the receiver report's slice counters step by (Receiver report, Open item 2) `[decision]`. A stream in the multi-packet regime states its frames per slice again in its regime stamp section (Slice header, Multi-packet regime); the two must agree, which no packet rule checks (Open item 9) `[decision]`. Controller bytes per slice is the controller-stream figure of C-219 that 010-04 reads from the manifest and that the commissioning configuration's `controller_bytes` copies from it (028a-03), so admission and the commissioning tool reach one declared rate (Size budget, Wire size and capacity recompute); the header part H is C-219's fixed 100 bytes and has no field `[decision]`.

### Join element

An inserted join delay is the whole-sample delay line the compiler put on the shorter path at a join of paths of different latency, with the flag C-104 sets on a join whose path has frequency-dependent delay `[decision]`:

| Offset (bytes) | Width (bytes) | Type | Meaning | Valid range |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 4 | u32 | Inserted join delay (C-104): the delay padded onto the shorter path at one join, in whole samples at the domain rate, 96 000 Hz (C-01, C-105) | 1 to L samples |
| 4 | 1 bit | uint | Frequency-dependent flag of the join, bit 0 of byte 4: 1 = a path of this join has frequency-dependent delay and was padded on its structural part only (C-104, C-189) | 0 or 1 |
| 4 | 7 bits | uint | Reserved, bits 7..1 of byte 4 (Reserved fields, General rule for later tickets) | 0 only |

A join element is 5 bytes, fixed `[decision]`. A delay is at least 1, because a join that needs no padding inserts nothing and is not listed, and at most L, because the padding brings the shorter path up to the longest path, whose latency is L (C-104) `[decision]`. A join opted out by the explicit construct of C-104 inserts nothing and is not listed; the frequency-dependent-delay property of an output (C-189) is this flag, as the ticket's rulings have it `[decision]`. At most 255 join delays per manifest; the bound is this section's, with its owner and falsifier in Open item 1 `[decision]`.

### Lists

The channel map, the dense initial values and the sparse initial values are three flat lists, each holding every stream's part in stream-element order — the first stream element's part first — and each stream's part is as long as that element's own count says `[decision]`. Flat lists keep the maximum a sum rather than a product: a channel map of up to 1 536 entries inside each of 256 stream elements would bound the manifest at 256 × 3 072 = 786 432 bytes for channel maps alone, above the 304 980 bytes the envelope can carry, while one list of 1 536 entries costs 3 072 `[decision]`.

- **Channel map (C-04).** Entry k of a stream's part names the port channel, an index into the channels of its output port, that travels as the stream's k-th channel run in the audio message, the map's first channel first (Content messages, Audio message) `[decision]`. Every channel of an output is named exactly once among the streams of that output: no entry is at or above the port's channel count, no port channel is named twice, and the port's channel count equals the number of entries its streams carry; this is 023-07's rule that each channel is mapped exactly once, written from the stream's side `[decision]`. The audio message's channel count must equal the stream's channel count (Content messages, Validity rules) `[decision]`.
- **Dense controller initial values (C-158).** A stream's part holds one value per dense controller in controller-map order: the controller map is that order, and the dense messages of a slice carry the stream's dense controllers in it (Dense controller message) `[decision]`. Each value is 4 bytes in the stream's sample format, the value used when a gap precedes any received value; a controller with no stated initial value is written 0, the default C-158 gives `[decision]`.
- **Sparse field map.** A stream's part holds one initial value per field of the stream's sparse field map; a field's id is its index within the stream's part, 0 first, so the map declares field ids 0 to the stream's sparse count − 1, at most 256, the 1-byte field id of the sparse keyed entry `[decision]`. Each value is 4 bytes in the stream's sample format: the value a field holds before any entry has reached it, and the value at which the sender may retire a pair (Sparse keyed entry; Sparse refresh obligation) `[decision]`.

### E and the cost bound

The unit of E is the nanosecond, and both E fields are u32 `[decision]`. E is defined by C-73 and its targets are given in microseconds (C-70 to C-72); nanoseconds hold every target exactly — 50 µs is 50 000 ns — and are the unit of the downstream A in the admission accept and of epic 012's presentation terms, whose `required_e_ns` and `sink_e_ns` are u32 nanoseconds (012-01, 012-07) `[decision]`. A u32 holds up to 4 294 967 293 ns as an E value, about 4.29 s, more than three orders of magnitude above the coarsest target, 1 ms (C-72) `[decision]`.

The two encodings are sentinels at the top of the u32 range, outside the range of every valid E value: 0xffffffff in the required E field means none, the group sets no required E (C-103); 0xfffffffe in the declared E field means unknown, the sink has no certified E (C-103, C-140) `[decision]`. Valid E values are 0 to 4 294 967 293 ns, so neither none nor unknown is a valid E value and no valid E value can be read as either; the two codes are distinct from each other as well, and each is invalid in the other field, so a field never carries the other field's meaning `[decision]`. Sentinels rather than a presence flag: a flag would add a byte whose value is undefined when it is clear, while a sentinel keeps one field with one meaning per value, the convention the accept's downstream A already follows with 0 for no A known `[decision]`.

The required E is the one of the module's own presentation group, the group whose A a consumer carries as its own (C-55; Admission messages, Downstream A): its manifest carries that group's required E, or none when the group sets none `[decision]`. The declared E is the module's own as a sink: a commissioned sink declares its certified E, and an uncertified or self-monitored sink declares unknown (C-140); a module with no device-facing output has no certified E as a sink and declares unknown too `[decision]`. Which side compares which values, and whom `RequiredEExceeded` refuses, is the admission machine's (005-14; Open item 3) `[decision]`.

The cost bound is a u64 of counted abstract operations per slice, the width the compiler's manifest and the ABI give it (023-16, 024a-01), with 0xffffffffffffffff for none: a module that is not a DSL program has no counted cost bound, which epic 012 holds as `None`, and a DSL consumer receiving none is refused with `CostBound` (012-03) `[decision]`. 012-01's `Declared` holds the bound as an optional u32 (Open item 7) `[decision]`.

### Maximum size and the envelope

The maximum manifest size, 18 977 bytes, is above the 1 252-byte control bound (C-17), and it travels as the landed envelope carries any manifest: split across manifest sections of at most 1 196 manifest bytes, each section at most 56 + 1 196 = 1 252 bytes (Admission messages, Manifest section) `[decision]`. At its maximum the manifest needs ⌈18 977 / 1 196⌉ = 16 sections, 15 of 1 252 bytes and one of 56 + 1 037 = 1 093 bytes, against the 255 the section count allows; the smallest manifest, 31 bytes, takes one section of 87 bytes `[decision]`.

**Falsified: the one-message manifest.** The computed maximum manifest, 18 977 bytes, exceeds 1 252 bytes, and this falsifies the design statements that assumed a manifest carried in one message of at most 1 252 bytes: the epic's AC-3, "every control message, every regime stamp section and every admission message including the manifest has a stated maximum size and none exceeds 1 252 bytes (C-17)", is falsified for the manifest, and 012-08 AC 2, "Encoded request with 32 members, 32 sessions and the full manifest fits in 1 252 bytes (C-17)", is falsified too, as is the second clause of this ticket's join-delay falsifier (Open item 1) `[decision]`. In the same finding, no numbered constant is falsified: C-17 bounds one control message, and each manifest section is within it, at most 56 + 1 196 = 1 252 bytes `[decision]`. The report goes to the design owner, to whom the lead reported it on 2026-11-16 `[decision]`. The proposed split is the landed manifest-section envelope, unchanged: the manifest in ⌈18 977 / 1 196⌉ = 16 sections at its maximum, within the 255 the section count allows, each section naming its handshake by the request id, its index and the section count (Admission messages, Manifest section) `[decision]`.

The size budget is not what bounds the join delays: with every other count at its maximum the manifest is 17 702 bytes, and the envelope's 255 × 1 196 = 304 980 bytes would hold ⌊(304 980 − 17 702) / 5⌋ = 57 455 join elements `[decision]`. The bound is 255, a one-byte count, because no constant bounds the joins of a module and 255 costs 1 275 bytes, two sections at most (Open item 1) `[decision]`.

A receiver that holds a peer's manifest whole needs at most 18 977 bytes for it; the manifest is built from fixed-width elements with fixed maxima, so a `no_std` parser bounds its work and its memory before it reads the first section (Field-table format, rule 2) `[decision]`.

### Which side sends it, and when

Proposed, for epic 005 to confirm (005-13), as the admission message set is: the source side sends its own manifest beside every admission request, in manifest sections carrying that request's request id (Admission messages, Who sends what) `[decision]`. This is admission-machine behaviour, not a wire rule; the wire layout implies only which decisions need the source's manifest `[decision]`. Whether a listed member is unknown — `BadMemberList` (012-05, C-173) — is decided against the output the source port id names, whose streams are the source manifest's stream elements that name it (Stream element), so that decision, and those that read the source's channel map, sample format, controller map or stream flags, wait until the consumer side has every section of the source's manifest; every other refusal does not wait: the `ControlDegraded` minimal path answers from the request's first 52 bytes (Admission messages, The refusal stays small), a member count of 33 to 255 is refused with `BadMemberList` from the count byte, `NotAnInput` reads the consumer's own manifest, and the rate checks read the request, all without the manifest `[decision]`. Which refusal is named when several apply is the admission machine's order (005-14, its Work 3; Open item 3) `[decision]`.

The sections of one manifest are those with one sender module id and one request id; once a section is present for each index from 0 to the section count − 1, the validity rules below run on the joined bytes `[decision]`. How long a consumer waits for missing sections, and what ends a handshake whose source manifest never completes or is dropped as invalid, are not wire fields: Pending has no timeout on the wire (C-45), and the rows that act on it are the admission machine's (005-14; Open item 3) `[decision]`. The consumer side's own manifest carries the E values that epic 012 places in the consumer's manifest (012-07), and the envelope lets either side send one; whether and when the consumer side sends it is epic 005's (005-13; Open item 3) `[decision]`.

### Validity rules

A manifest that breaks a rule below is dropped whole — every section of it held so far — and counted once under the named member of the closed Rejection reasons enumeration, per (socket, reason) on the receiving control socket, as every admission message is (Admission messages, Validity rules); an identical repeat of a section already held is the one form that is not a rejection: it is ignored and the held section kept `[decision]`. The first three rows are reassembly rules: each is decided when a section arrives, against the sections already held for the same sender module id and request id, which is per-handshake state the receiver keeps, not a single datagram's bytes; an identical repeat follows the merged duplicate rules — a duplicate is valid and the first copy wins (Receiver report, Report number; Sync request and reply, Exchange rules; media rule 9) — so a sender may resend a section without harm, while sections that disagree are this section's BadLength drops; what the admission machine does after a drop — a resend, a refusal, or a wait — is 005-13's and 005-14's (Open item 3) `[decision]`. The remaining rows run on the joined bytes once every index is present, in the order of the table, each decidable in one pass, linear in the manifest's length (C-164); the first rule that applies decides the reason `[decision]`:

| Invalid form | Required outcome |
|---|---|
| duplicate section index: a second section of one manifest (one sender module id, one request id) with a section index already held, the same section count and the same manifest length and bytes | not a rejection: the repeat is ignored and counted under no Reject reason, and the section already held is kept, as merged text treats every duplicate as valid — a duplicated receiver report is "a valid message, never a rejection" (Receiver report, Report number), a duplicate sync reply "a valid exchange" (Sync request and reply, Exchange rules), and of media copies "the first valid copy wins" (media rule 9; Reserved fields, Copy index (C-07)) `[decision]` |
| sections disagree on the section count: a section of one manifest states a section count different from a section already held | drop the manifest and count under BadLength: the sections do not form one declared shape `[decision]` |
| sections disagree on the manifest length: a section of one manifest whose section index is already held carries a different manifest length or different manifest bytes | drop the manifest and count under BadLength: the sections do not form one declared shape `[decision]` |
| manifest size below the 31-byte fixed part | drop the manifest and count under BadLength: the body does not fill the fixed size `[decision]` |
| a count above its maximum: port count above 51, bundle count above 8, stream count above 256, channel-map entry count above 1 536, dense controller count above 1 024 or sparse field count above 1 024; in an element, a name length of 0 or above 32, a content-type count of 0 or above 4, a member count of 0 or above 32, or a stream's sparse field count above 256 | drop the manifest and count under BadLength: a count outside its fixed range `[decision]` |
| length inconsistent: the manifest size is not 31 + the sum over the port elements of 9 + content-type count + name length + the sum over the bundle elements of 2 + member count + 15 × stream count + 5 × join-delay count + 2 × channel-map entry count + 4 × (dense controller count + sparse field count), or the stream elements' own counts do not sum to the fixed part's three totals | drop the manifest and count under BadLength: the body does not fill the size the counts declare `[decision]` |
| reserved non-zero: a set bit in bits 7..2 of a port element's byte 0, bits 7..4 of a stream element's byte 2 or bits 7..1 of a join element's byte 4 | drop the manifest and count under ReservedNonZero (Reserved fields, General rule for later tickets) `[decision]` |
| a conversion delay on a port that is not device-facing: a non-zero conversion delay in a port element whose device-facing bit is 0 (C-207) | drop the manifest and count under BadLength: the field disagrees with the port's declared shape `[decision]` |
| channel-map entry out of range — at or above the channel count of its stream's output port — or duplicated — one port channel named twice among the streams of one output — or an output whose channel count differs from the number of channel-map entries its streams carry (C-04) | drop the manifest and count under BadLength: the entries disagree with the shape the ports declare `[decision]` |
| L above 67 108 863 samples, or an inserted join delay of 0 or above L | drop the manifest and count under BadLength `[decision]` |
| required E of 0xfffffffe, or declared E of 0xffffffff: the other field's code | drop the manifest and count under BadLength `[decision]` |
| two stream elements with one stream number; an output port id naming no port of the manifest, an input or a device-facing port; two bundle elements with one group id; a member that is no stream element of the manifest, a stream listed twice in one bundle or in two bundles, or a bundle whose members name different output ports; one content type listed twice in a port | drop the manifest and count under BadLength: the elements disagree with the shape the table declares `[decision]` |
| role inconsistent: an audio stream with a channel count of 0; a controller stream with a channel count above 0, or with no dense controller, no sparse field and no transport state; an audio member of a bundle with a dense controller, a sparse field, the transport flag or controller bytes (C-205) | drop the manifest and count under BadLength: the stream disagrees with its declared role `[decision]` |
| frames per slice of 0 | drop the manifest and count under BadLength: a count outside its fixed range `[decision]` |
| audio length above its bound: a stream whose frames per slice × channel count × 4 exceeds 65 535, the audio message's length maximum (Content messages, Audio message) | drop the manifest and count under BadLength: the stream has no v1 wire form `[decision]` |
| port name not valid UTF-8 (RFC 3629) | drop the manifest and count under BadLength `[decision]` — provisional, as for the hello's name (Hello, Validity rules); confirmed by 004-21 (Open item 2) |

Not a rejection: an identical repeated section is ignored (the first row); a content-type code above 3 is ignored, as in the hello, and the port keeps its other codes; no value of a group id, a conversion delay on a device-facing port, a controller-bytes figure, a cost bound or an initial value is a malformed case, since each accepts every value its type holds `[decision]`. What the admission machine does with a handshake whose peer manifest was dropped is its own (005-14; Open item 3); the Rejection reasons enumeration is unchanged, and every outcome above except the identical repeat names an existing member whose Layers cell lists the control layer `[decision]`.

### Open items

**Open item 1 — the bound on inserted join delays.** No constant bounds the number of inserted join delays (C-104); this section fixes the bound at 255 join delays per manifest, a one-byte count and at most 1 275 bytes of join elements, and a module with more padded joins has no manifest form: as a DSL program it is refused at compile time by the DSL front end (C-218). Owner: epic 004 (this ticket, 004-26), with epic 023, which implements compile-time rejections (C-218). Falsifier, as the ticket words it: a compiled module has more padded joins than the bound, or the manifest then exceeds 1 252 bytes. The second clause is already met at the maximum counts, whatever the join bound: the manifest exceeds 1 252 bytes through its ports, streams, channel map and initial values even with no join (Maximum size and the envelope, Falsified: the one-message manifest), so only the first clause can still falsify the bound `[decision]`.

**Open item 2 — the manifest's maxima and one provisional outcome.** No constant bounds the ports, names, bundles, channels, controllers or sparse fields of a manifest, or its L; this section fixes 51 ports and 8 bundles (the hello's), names of 1 to 32 bytes, 256 streams (C-09), 256 sparse fields per stream (the 1-byte field id), and an L of at most 67 108 863 samples (the measurement extension's bound) `[decision]`. Three maxima are chosen values, not derived from a constant `[decision]`. 1 536 channel-map entries per manifest is C-09's 256 streams at C-04's default 6 channels, the arithmetic of C-209's 186 channels, chosen although the wire admits wider streams — up to 341 channels at the default 48 frames (Content messages, Audio message) — so a source the wire admits with more than 1 536 channels in all has no manifest form `[decision]`. 1 024 dense controllers and 1 024 sparse fields per manifest are chosen as four times what one stream can name in one place, the 255 controllers of one dense message and the 256 field ids of one sparse map, rounded to a power of two, with no constant behind them `[decision]`. A module beyond any maximum has no manifest form and is refused at compile time (C-218) or refuses to start. This section does not answer 023-07's gap — whether a member may be wider than 6 channels — beyond what the wire already admits per stream; it stays with epic 004 (004-22). An invalid UTF-8 port name is counted under BadLength provisionally, as the hello's name is. Owner: epic 004 (this ticket, then 004-22 for 023-07's gap), with epic 023 for the compile-time rejections (C-218), and ticket 004-21 (validity rules completeness audit) for the provisional outcome. Falsifier: a module that must run on the wire needs more ports, bundles, channels in all (above 1 536), dense controllers or sparse fields than these maxima, or an L above 67 108 863 samples; or 004-21 finds a member of the closed enumeration that names an invalid port name better `[decision]`.

**Open item 3 — which side sends its manifest, and what waits for it.** Proposed: the source side sends its manifest beside every request; the decisions that read it — an unknown member (`BadMemberList`), the channel map and the stream flags — wait for every section of it, and every other refusal does not wait (ControlDegraded, a member count of 33 or more, NotAnInput, the rates); nothing on the wire bounds the wait, and what ends a handshake whose source manifest never completes, is dropped as invalid, or loses a section to a reassembly rule is not decided here. A request whose consumer port is a device-facing input is refused by no landed rule (012-05). Epic 012 places required E and declared E in the consumer's manifest (012-07) and has the consumer compare them itself (012-13), which needs no manifest on the wire, while ADR 12 and 005-13 put E in the manifest exchange; whether and when the consumer side sends its manifest is open. 012-08's AC 2 expects the full manifest inside a request of at most 1 252 bytes, which the landed message set and this layout do not have. Owner: ticket 005-13 (admission handshake states and inputs), which confirms who sends a manifest and when, with 005-14 (admission handshake transition table) for the wait and the refusal order, 012-05 (manifest and bundle member checks), 012-12 and 012-13 (admission engines) and 012-08 (handshake message codec). Falsifier: a consumer must decide membership before the source's manifest has arrived; a check of the source side needs the consumer's manifest and no rule sends it; or a handshake whose manifest is lost or dropped never ends `[decision]`.

**Open item 4 — port names before a handshake.** The port names are in the manifest (Port element), and the manifest travels only in manifest sections, each of which names a handshake by its request id; no message of the landed set and no field of this layout lets a module read a peer's manifest outside a handshake, and this section does not invent one. Hello, Open item 9's falsifier — neither the manifest nor the handshake lets a requester learn a port's name and id before it names the port — therefore holds today. Owner: the design owner, with ticket 004-18 (admission message set; codes 20 to 31 are unused in v1) and 005-13, and 028a-03 (commissioning configuration), which decides how a configuration names ports. Falsifier: a requester or the commissioning configuration must name a port by its name before any handshake, and no message gives it the port id for that name `[decision]`.

**Open item 5 — what the conversion delay counts.** The conversion delay is the fixed-ratio resampler node's delay in whole domain-rate samples, 0 for a device at 96 000 Hz, as 021-14, 023-29, 024a-12 and 024b-05 have it; 018-11 and 018-27 count the rate actuator's delay inside the manifest's conversion delay, in nanoseconds, non-zero at 96 000 Hz. Owner: ticket 018-27 (conversion delay in the manifest), with 021-14 (declared D from device properties) and 023-29 (edge modules and single wire rate). Falsifier: an edge's D computed from the manifest's conversion delay plus its actuator's own delay differs from the D the edge declares, or a device-facing port needs a conversion delay that is not a whole number of domain-rate samples `[decision]`.

**Open item 6 — channel names and controller value ranges.** The Hello section says the full name of a port or channel is in the manifest; this layout carries port names and no channel names, because 1 536 channel names of up to 33 bytes would add 50 688 bytes, and a channel is identified by its output port and its index in the channel map. Content messages say that how a controller's or a sparse field's natural range maps onto Q1.31's [−1, +1) is agreed through the manifest; this layout carries no range. Owner: epic 004 (004-22, review against the architecture specification), with 023-19 (the compiler's manifest) and 028a-03 (commissioning configuration). Falsifier: the commissioning configuration or an operator tool must address a channel by name, or a Q1.31 consumer must know a controller's range to use its values, and neither can be read from any manifest `[decision]`.

**Open item 7 — the compiler's manifest and the wire manifest.** The compiler's `Manifest` (023-19) and its binary form (023-21, 024b-12) hold more than admission reads: the module name (in the hello), the ABI version, state size, stack bound, determinism class (whose effective value the regime stamp carries, 01-architecture.md § 12.5), gap overrides, L and cost per output, joins opted out and join ids (023-28, 024b-06), the controller placement and whether the compiler forced it (023-31; here implied by the stream elements' roles and ports), admission figures and conformance vectors; this layout carries the admission set the ticket lists. 012-01's `Declared` carries a buffer memory, which the receiver computes from A and the rates (C-96) and no manifest field holds, and a cost bound as an optional u32, while this layout and 023-16 use a u64. Owner: ticket 023-21 (manifest binary form), with 023-19, and 012-01 (core admission types) for the buffer memory and the cost bound's width. Falsifier: admission or a peer needs, at handshake time, an item of the compiler's manifest this layout lacks; or a cost bound above 4 294 967 295 operations per slice, which 012-01's u32 cannot hold `[decision]`.

**Open item 8 — whose initial values.** This layout declares the initial value of each dense controller in the producing stream's part, in controller-map order, as Dense controller message reads C-158; 023-19 lists the initial value of each dense controller input in the consumer's own manifest, and 009-19 conceals at the consumer with "the manifest's declared initial value". Owner: ticket 009-19 (default concealment), with 023-19 (manifest type). Falsifier: a consumer must conceal a controller with an initial value that its own manifest declares and that differs from the producer's `[decision]`.

**Open item 9 — frames per slice in two places.** The stream element states each stream's frames per slice, and a stream in the multi-packet regime states its frames per slice in its regime stamp section too (Slice header, Multi-packet regime); this section does not say which wins if they differ. Owner: ticket 004-25 (regime stamp sections), which lays out the multi-packet regime section. Falsifier: a stream's regime stamp and its manifest state different frames per slice for one stream `[decision]`. Status, 2026-11-16: decided in Regime stamp, Multi-packet regime section: for an admitted connection the manifest's frames per slice is authoritative, the stamp restates it, and it changes only through a re-admission with a new manifest `[decision]`.

## Path-MTU probe pair

The path-MTU probe pair is how the admission handshake learns whether a connection's path carries the connection's media packets whole: the source side sends a padded probe at the intended media packet size (C-116), the consumer side answers it with a small fixed-size reply, and a missing reply means the path is smaller (01-architecture.md § 3.3) `[decision]`. When the probe at the intended size gets no reply, the source side probes again at the 1 280-byte fallback (C-06), which is 1 252 bytes of UDP payload over IPv4 (C-17, C-100); when that probe gets no reply either, the handshake is refused with `PathMtuTooSmall` (C-173) `[decision]`. This section gives the pair its two codes in the block the Hello section reserves, lays out both messages, states their sizes, how a reply names its probe, the sequence and every invalid form with its outcome `[decision]`. It is not the one-way probe of the measurement layer (One-way probe), which measures delay between modules that already share an admitted connection; every name here says "path-MTU probe" to keep the two apart `[decision]`.

### Exchange

Who probes and in which direction `[decision]`: the source side probes and the consumer side answers. Media flows from the source to the consumer, so the forward path from the source side's pinned source address is the path whose size matters; the source side alone knows the intended packet size, which it puts in its admission request (Admission messages, Admission request), and the landed handshake already has the source side refuse an accepted handshake in turn with `PathMtuTooSmall` (Admission messages, Who sends what). The source side sends the first probe once it has sent its admission request, as 012-12 sends the request and the first probe together; the consumer answers every well-formed probe addressed to it when it receives it, whatever its own verdict on the request, so the probe and the request may cross on the wire in either order `[decision]`.

Addressing and marking `[decision]`: a probe travels by UDP unicast over IPv4 (C-100), from the pinned source address the request states to the address and port the source side sends its admission messages to; the reply travels from the address the probe arrived on to the source address and port of the probe, the addressing a stateless reply needs, as the one-way probe uses (One-way probe, Exchange rules). Both messages are control messages at DSCP CS5 (40) (C-13), with the common control header (Hello, Common control header) and the C-12 authentication reservation at the v1 values of C-115: scheme 0, tag 128 zero bits and a live message counter, the sender's liveness counter (Hello, Liveness counter) `[decision]`.

Don't-fragment (rule 11) `[decision]`: the sender sets the IPv4 don't-fragment bit on every probe and every reply, as on every packet of the protocol, and IP fragmentation is forbidden; without it a probe could arrive in fragments and prove nothing. An IPv4 datagram with the DF bit set is not fragmented: RFC 791, § 3.1 Internet Header Format, Flags: "Bit 1: (DF) 0 = May Fragment, 1 = Don't Fragment"; § 2.3: a datagram marked don't fragment that cannot be delivered without fragmenting "is to be discarded instead" `[standard]`. The protocol relies on that discard and on nothing else: it treats only the missing reply as the signal that the path is smaller, and it does not rely on an ICMP message reporting the drop, because on a switched path a too-small hop drops silently and silence is the answer only because a reply is expected (01-architecture.md § 3.3; 012-09) `[decision]`. The DF bit is in the IPv4 header, outside these messages, so no validity rule checks it; how a sender that cannot set it behaves is not specified anywhere in the design: the platform tickets 001-04, 001-09 and 021-03 test whether the bit can be set, and none says what a sender does when it cannot (Open item 6) `[decision]`.

The fallback sequence, in order `[decision]`:

1. The first probe is sent at the request's intended packet size (C-116), or at 54 bytes, the probe's fixed part, when the intended size is smaller: a path that carries 54 bytes carries any smaller packet `[decision]`.
2. If the intended packet size is above 1 252 bytes and the first probe gets no reply, the source side sends a second probe at the 1 280-byte fallback (C-06): 1 252 bytes of UDP payload, 1 280 bytes at the IP layer once the 20-byte IPv4 header and the 8-byte UDP header are added (Layer overview) `[decision]`.
3. If the probe at the fallback also gets no reply, the source side refuses the handshake with `PathMtuTooSmall` (C-173), in an admission refusal carrying the request's request id (Admission messages, Admission refusal) `[decision]`.

When the intended packet size is 1 252 bytes or less, the first probe is the only one: it is no larger than the fallback, so it is the probe at the fallback, and if it gets no reply the handshake is refused with `PathMtuTooSmall` `[decision]`. This is the reading 012-09 records (its AC 4: a first probe at or below 1 280 bytes counts as the fallback probe), and a second probe at 1 252 bytes would only test a size the connection never sends: the intended packet size is the largest media packet the source sends on the connection (Admission messages, Admission request), so a path that carries it carries every packet of the connection `[decision]`. This answers the Size budget's open item on the probe size (Size budget, Announcement schedule and omission rule): either the probe at the fallback carries 1 252 bytes of UDP payload with don't-fragment set, or the only probe carries the intended packet size, the largest media packet the connection sends, so no probe is sized below the connection's largest packet `[decision]`. The default slice is that case: it fits 1 252 bytes of UDP payload (C-137), so a path that passes the fallback probe carries the default slice, and every v1 default packet is at most 1 252 bytes (Size budget, Shared packets and the controller allowance) `[decision]`. What a reply proves, for the source side, is a lower bound only: a reply to a probe of intended packet size s shows that the path carries datagrams of at least s bytes of UDP payload, s + 28 bytes at the IP layer, in the direction the probe travels — from the source side to the consumer, the direction media flows — and nothing about how much larger a datagram it would carry, nor anything about the reverse direction beyond the reply's own 54 bytes (Path-MTU probe reply) `[decision]`. A reply to the first probe therefore means every packet of the connection crosses whole; a reply only to the probe at the fallback means the path carries 1 252 bytes of UDP payload but, as far as the pair shows, not the intended size, so a slice that no longer fits one packet travels in the explicit, stamped multi-packet regime (C-05; Slice header, Multi-packet regime); no reply to the probe at the fallback means `PathMtuTooSmall` `[decision]`. Which value, if any, the connection records as its path MTU is a separate question that this section does not answer: 013-07 stamps a `path_mtu` per connection and its AC 4 lowers one from 1 500 to 1 280, while 012-09 names an outcome per probe, and neither says how a recorded value derives from a lower bound (Open item 2) `[decision]`.

How many times a probe of one size is sent before it counts as unanswered, and how long the source side waits for a reply, are not wire fields and no constant gives them: 012-09 proposes a timeout as a pending-measurement parameter and sends one probe per size, and the probe retry timing is epic 005's, outside this ticket (Open item 1) `[decision]`. Where the result travels: in no admission message — the accept is the consumer's and carries no path MTU (Admission messages, Admission accept) — but in each side's regime stamp, which records the path MTU of each connection (01-architecture.md § 3.3; C-18), in a section no landed ticket of this epic lays out yet (Open item 2) `[decision]`.

### Path-MTU probe request

The probe request is the padded probe, code 32 (Hello, Control message type codes). Little-endian (Byte order), packed with no padding for alignment (Alignment and padding); bytes 0 to 31 are the common control header, and bytes 32 to 51 are the three fields every admission message carries at those offsets — sender, recipient and request id (Admission messages, Common admission fields) — so one parser reads the handshake's addressing alike in all six messages `[decision]`:

| Offset (bytes) | Width (bytes) | Type | Meaning | Valid range |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 2 | u16 | Magic 0x4d4e, the ASCII letters M and N, fixed by 004-04 | 0x4d4e only |
| 2 | 2 | u16 | Protocol version, major in the high byte (bits 15..8), minor in the low byte; v1 packs 1.0 as 0x0100 (004-04) | major 1, any minor (Versioning, Receiver behaviour); senders write 0x0100 |
| 4 | 2 | u16 | Domain id (C-11); 0 is the unconfigured domain | 1 to 65535; 0 = unconfigured, ignored and counted (C-173) |
| 6 | 1 | u8 | Message type: 32 = path-MTU probe request (Control message type codes) | 32 only |
| 7 | 1 | u8 | Authentication scheme (C-12); 0 = none in v1 (C-115) | 0 only |
| 8 | 8 | u64 | Message counter (C-12): live (C-115), the sender's data-plane liveness counter (Hello, Liveness counter) | any 64-bit value |
| 16 | 16 | u128 | Authentication tag (C-12): 128 zero bits in v1 (C-115) | 0 only |
| 32 | 8 | u64 | Sender module id (C-11): the source side's module id, the prober | any 64-bit value |
| 40 | 8 | u64 | Recipient module id (C-11): the consumer module the probe is for | the receiving module's own module id |
| 48 | 4 | u32 | Request id: the request id of the admission request whose path this probe tests (Admission messages, Common admission fields); with the intended packet size it identifies the probe | any 32-bit value |
| 52 | 2 | u16 | Intended packet size: the UDP payload size this probe is sent at, and so the datagram's own size — the request's intended packet size for the first probe (C-116), or 54 when that is smaller; 1 252 for the probe at the fallback (C-06) | 54 to 65 507 |
| 54 | 1 × max 65453 | pad | Padding: intended packet size − 54 bytes, which bring the datagram to the intended packet size; senders write zero (Reserved fields, General rule for later tickets) | 0 only; intended packet size − 54 bytes, at most 65 453 |

The request's maximum size is 65 507 bytes: 32 + 8 + 8 + 4 + 2 = 54 bytes of fixed part and 65 453 bytes of padding at the most, 54 + 65 453 = 65 507, tiling bytes 0 to 65 506 with no gap; a probe of intended packet size s is s bytes, at least 54 `[decision]`. The padding is a counted field whose count derives from the intended packet size, as the audio message's samples derive from its length field (Content messages, Audio message): the size field is the only length the probe needs, so no separate padding length is carried `[decision]`. The padding carries no content of its own, only zero bytes; it is the general rule's padding, so the Reserved fields section gains no row, as the one-way probe's padding gains none (One-way probe, Request) `[decision]`.

The maximum is the largest UDP payload an IPv4 datagram can carry, and the largest intended packet size the admission request admits (Admission messages, Admission request: 1 to 65 507) `[decision]`. The IPv4 Total Length is 16 bits and counts the whole datagram, header included, so a datagram is at most 65 535 bytes — RFC 791, § 3.1 Internet Header Format, Total Length: "This field allows the length of a datagram to be up to 65,535 octets" — and the UDP header is 8 bytes of it (RFC 768, Fields, Length: the length counts the header and the data, so its minimum is eight) `[standard]`; 65 535 − 20 − 8 = 65 507 `[decision]`. A probe is sent at the request's intended packet size, so it can be no smaller a bound than the request's; the paths the design budgets carry at most 1 472 bytes of UDP payload, at a 1 500-byte path MTU (Size budget), and a probe above that size gets a reply only on a path every hop of which carries larger frames, which the design does not assume — jumbo frames are a rejected default (ADR 19) — so on such a path the probe at the fallback decides `[decision]`. A receiver whose receive buffer is smaller than a probe reads a datagram shorter than its intended packet size and drops it (Validity rules), and the source side then falls back exactly as on a smaller path `[decision]`.

### Path-MTU probe reply

The probe reply is the consumer side's answer, code 33: the probe's fixed part only, with the sender and the recipient swapped and the request id and the intended packet size copied verbatim from the probe, so a responder builds it from those two fields of the probe and needs no state `[decision]`:

| Offset (bytes) | Width (bytes) | Type | Meaning | Valid range |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 2 | u16 | Magic 0x4d4e, the ASCII letters M and N, fixed by 004-04 | 0x4d4e only |
| 2 | 2 | u16 | Protocol version, major in the high byte (bits 15..8), minor in the low byte; v1 packs 1.0 as 0x0100 (004-04) | major 1, any minor (Versioning, Receiver behaviour); senders write 0x0100 |
| 4 | 2 | u16 | Domain id (C-11); 0 is the unconfigured domain | 1 to 65535; 0 = unconfigured, ignored and counted (C-173) |
| 6 | 1 | u8 | Message type: 33 = path-MTU probe reply (Control message type codes) | 33 only |
| 7 | 1 | u8 | Authentication scheme (C-12); 0 = none in v1 (C-115) | 0 only |
| 8 | 8 | u64 | Message counter (C-12): live (C-115), the sender's data-plane liveness counter (Hello, Liveness counter) | any 64-bit value |
| 16 | 16 | u128 | Authentication tag (C-12): 128 zero bits in v1 (C-115) | 0 only |
| 32 | 8 | u64 | Sender module id (C-11): the consumer side's module id, the responder | any 64-bit value |
| 40 | 8 | u64 | Recipient module id (C-11): the prober, the sender module id of the probe this reply answers | the receiving module's own module id |
| 48 | 4 | u32 | Request id: the probe's request id, echoed verbatim | any 32-bit value |
| 52 | 2 | u16 | Echoed intended packet size: the probe's intended packet size, echoed verbatim, at the request's width; it names which probe of the handshake this reply acknowledges | any 16-bit value as far as parsing goes; a value no outstanding probe carries matches none (Matching a reply to its probe) |

The reply's maximum size is 54 bytes, which is also its fixed size: 32 + 8 + 8 + 4 + 2 = 54, tiling bytes 0 to 53 with no gap, far within the 1 252-byte control bound (C-17); it carries no padding, whatever the size of the probe it acknowledges `[decision]`.

A reply therefore proves the path only in the direction the probe travels, from the source side to the consumer, which is the direction media flows; the reverse direction is tested only at the reply's 54 bytes, and the reverse path's own messages — receiver reports, one-way probes and admission messages — all fit 1 252 bytes, the bound C-17 sets for control messages and Receiver report, Size bound of the measurement layer adopts for reports and one-way probes, because a connection may run on the fallback path `[decision]`. Nothing in the pair tests that the reverse direction carries those 1 252 bytes: on a path whose reverse direction carries less than the control bound, the connection is admitted without any PathMtuTooSmall, and every receiver report (up to 1 222 bytes, Receiver report, Maximum size) or manifest section larger than that reverse path carries is then lost, so the producer times out after 2 s without receiver reports (C-41) (Open item 7) `[decision]`. 012-13's "acknowledged with size s" is read here as an acknowledgement carrying the value s, as 012-09's `on_ack(now_ns, size: u16)` takes it (Open item 5) `[decision]`.

### Matching a reply to its probe

A probe is identified by its request id together with its intended packet size: the request id names the handshake (Admission messages, Common admission fields), and the size tells the probe at the intended size from the probe at the fallback, the two probes one handshake can send `[decision]`. A reply matches an outstanding probe of the receiving module when its sender module id is the module id the probe was sent to, its echoed request id is that probe's request id and its echoed intended packet size is that probe's size; its recipient module id is the receiving module's own by the parse rules below `[decision]`. The echoed intended packet size is not a parse rule: every 16-bit value parses, and a value that no outstanding probe carries — one below 54 included — simply matches none `[decision]`. Matching needs the source side's per-handshake state — which probes it has sent and not yet decided — which no packet rule holds, so a reply that matches no outstanding probe is not a parse rejection: the reply is well formed, and the admission machine decides what to do with it, as it decides for an accept, refusal or manifest section whose request id names no handshake (Admission messages, Validity rules; 005-14) `[decision]`. An acknowledgement whose echoed size is not the size of an outstanding probe is ignored, as 012-09 has it ("an ack for a different size or after the outcome is ignored"); whether a late reply to the probe at the intended size may still lift a connection off the fallback is that machine's, with the re-probe of 012-10 `[decision]`. The measurement layer's UnadmittedSource is not used: its Layers cell does not list the control layer, and a probe precedes any admitted connection `[decision]`.

The responder needs no match: it answers every well-formed probe whose recipient module id is its own, whether or not it already holds a handshake under that request id, copying the probe's request id and intended packet size into the reply, so a probe that overtakes its request is answered `[decision]`. A reply is 54 bytes whatever the probe's size, so answering a probe never sends more bytes than the probe brought `[decision]`.

### Sizes and the exemption

By the design owner's decision of 2026-11-16, the reply is a small acknowledgement and only the probe is padded: the probe request alone is exempt from the 1 252-byte control bound (C-17) under C-116, read as the probe being sent at the intended media packet size, and it is the only exemption in the control layer `[decision]`.

In wire.toml only the probe request carries exempt_from_control_bound = "C-116", the attribute the 004-02 checker accepts and the only value it accepts; the reply does not carry it `[decision]`. The request is 54 to 65 507 bytes, and the reply is 54 bytes; the probe at the fallback is 1 252 bytes, within the control bound, and only a probe at an intended size above 1 252 bytes is larger `[decision]`. A probe request larger than 1 252 bytes is therefore never counted under OversizeControl: the message type is read before the size bound (Validity rules), and for code 32 that bound does not apply; a reply longer than its fixed 54 bytes is dropped under BadLength before the size bound is reached `[decision]`. Codes 34 and 35, the rest of the block, are unused in v1 `[decision]`.

### Validity rules

Every malformed probe or reply is dropped — or ignored, for the unconfigured domain — and counted under one member of the closed Rejection reasons enumeration; nothing is dropped silently, and a dropped probe is not answered `[decision]`.
